Financial instruments

In January 2016, the FASB issued ASU No. 2016-01, Recognition and Measurement of
Financial Assets and Financial Liabilities (ASU 2016-01”), which changes the accounting
for equity investments, and the presentation and disclosure for all financial instruments.
Among other changes, ASU 201 6-01 requires that the change in fair value of equity
secutities be tecognized in excess of expenses over revenue. The ASU is effective for
yeats beginning after December 15, 2018. After the adoption of ASU 2016-01, as
amended, the Home will include unrealized gains and losses atising from the changes in
fait values of equity securities during the petiod as a component of investment income
in the consolidated statement of operations.

Presentation of restricted cash

In November 2016, the FASB issued ASU No. 2016-18, Statement of Cash Flows (Topic
230): Restricted Cash (a consensus of the Emerging Issues Task Force) ("ASU 2016-18"), which
tequires an entity to present restricted cash and cash equivalents together with
unrestricted cash and cash equivalents in the statement of cash flows. Under ASU
2016-18, an entity will be tequited to disclose in the financial statements how the
statement of cash flows reconciles to the balance sheet, if testticted cash and cash
equivalents ate presented separate from untestticted cash and cash equivalents on the
balance sheet, and the nature of the testrictions at the balance sheet date. This new
guidance is effective for yeats beginning after December 15, 2018, with early adoption
petmitted. Upon adoption of the amendments in ASU 2016-18, the Home will present
within the consolidated statement of cash flows the change during the petiod in total
cash and cash equivalents, including amounts generally described as restricted cash and
cash equivalents. The amendments will be applied tetrospectively.

SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES (CONTINUED)

Basis of presentation

The consolidated financial statements of the Home have been ptepated in accordance
with U.S. GAAP, which require the Home to report information regarding its financial
position and activities according to the following net asset classifications:

Net Assets Withont Donor Restrictions - Net assets available for use in general opetations
and not subject to donot (ot certain grantor) restrictions.

Net Assets With Donor Restrictions - Net assets subject to donor-imposed testtictions.
Some donot-imposed testrictions ate temporary in natute, such as those that will be met
by the passage of time ot othet events specified by the donot. Other donot-imposed
restrictions ate perpetual in natute, where the donot stipulates that resources be
maintained in perpetuity. Gifts of long-lived assets and gifts of cash restricted for the
acquisition of long-lived assets are recognized as revenue when the assets ate placed in
setvice. Net assets with donor testtictions at December 31, 2018, ate restricted for the
memotial garden.

The Home reports contributions testticted by donors as increases in net assets without
donor restrictions if the testtictions expire (that is, when a stipulated time restriction
ends of putpose resttiction is accomplished) in the teporting petiod in which the
revenue is recognized. When a restriction expites, net assets with donor restrictions are
reclassified to net assets without donor restrictions and tepotted in the consolidated
statement of operations as net assets teleased from resttictions.

Revenue, gains and other support ate reported as increases in net assets without donor
testrictions unless use of the related assets is limited by donor-imposed testrictions or
by law. Expenses ate reported as decteases in net assets without donot testtictions.
Gains and losses on investments ot other assets ot liabilities ate reported as increases or
decteases in net assets without donor restrictions unless their use is restricted by explicit
donot stipulation ot by law.

Excess of expenses over tevenue

The consolidated statement of operations includes excess of expenses ovet revenue.
Changes in net assets without donot testtictions which ate excluded from excess of
expefises over tevenue, consistent with industry practice, include the change in net
untealized gains on other than trading secutities.

Conttibutions, donor-imposed testrictions and net assets released from restrictions

Contribution revenue is recorded as teceived or unconditionally promised. If a

contribution is made in assets other than cash, the amount of the contribution is |
measured at the fait value of the asset at the date the contribution or unconditional ‘
ptomise to give is made by the donor. "

Contributed services

Contributed setvices received by the Home ate not recognized as support since such
setvices either do not requite specialized skills or would not typically be purchased if not
ptovided by donation.

Net patient setvice revenue

Net patient service revenue is reported at estimated net amounts realizable from
tesidents, third-party payors (principally "Rhode Island Medicaid") and others for

services tendered.

‘The Home's third-party revenue is paid principally at ptospectively determined rates.
Retroactive adjustments ate considered in the recognition of revenue on an estimated
basis in the petiod the related services ate tendered; any differences between the
estimated amounts accrued and interim and final settlements are adjusted in future
petiods as adjustments become known or as yeats ate no longer subject to such audits,
reviews and investigations.

Laws and tegulations governing Medicate, Medicaid and other governmental
reimbursement ptogtams ate extremely complex and subject to interpretation. As a
result, there is at least a teasonable possibility that recorded estimates could change by a
material amount in the neat term. It is management's opinion that the Home is in
compliance with applicable laws and regulations; however, the possibility of government
teview and intetpretation exists. Compliance with such laws and regulations can be
subject to future government review and interpretation, as well as significant regulatory
action; failure to comply with such laws and regulations can tesult in fines, penalties and
exclusion from Medicate and Medicaid programs.

Net patient service revenue from Rhode Island Medicaid totaled $4,976,000 for the yeat
ended December 31, 2018.

Accounts receivable

Patient accounts receivable ate stated at the amount the Home expects to collect.

Under Rhode Island General Law Title 40, Chapter 8, Section 6.1(b)(2), Human Services,
Medical Assistance, Provider Care During Pendency of Application, Uncompensated Care While
Determination is Overdue, upon the Home’s tequest, the State of Rhode Island (the
“State”) is requited to make payments to the Home for chatges telated to a patient that
the Home has detetmined should be eligible for Medicaid and for whom a completed
application (as defined) for Medicaid benefits filed with the State has been pending for
90 days ot more (“advance payments”); such advance payments may not be tecoveted,
offset ot tecouped by the State upon a subsequent determination by the State that the
patient is not eligible for Medicaid. Consequently, advance payments received by the
Home prior to the date the State has tmade a determination telated to the patient’s
eligibility for Medicaid are offset against the patient setvices receivable in the
accompanying consolidated balance sheet. At December 31, 2018, advance payments
offset against patient services receivables totaled $81,000. Advance payments teceived
by the Home telated to Medicaid claims subsequently approved and paid by the State
ate tequired to be repaid to the State through an upcoming reconciliation process and,
consequently, are reported within cutrent liabilities, third-party advances in the
accompanying consolidated balance sheet. The Home repaid $336,000 to the State
during 2018.

Fait value measurements

FASB Accounting Standards Codification ("ASC") 820, Fair Value Measurement,
establishes a framework for measuting fair value. That framework provides a fair value
hierarchy that prioritizes the inputs to valuation techniques used to measute fair value.
The hierarchy gives the highest priority to unadjusted quoted prices in active markets
for identical assets ot liabilities (Level 1) and the lowest priority to unobservable inputs
(Level 3). Categorization within the valuation hierarchy is based upon the lowest level of
input that is significant to the fait value measurement. Valuation techniques used need
to maximize the use of observable inputs and minimize the use of unobservable inputs.
Under the standard, fair value is defined as the exit ptice, or the amount that would be
teceived to sell an asset ot paid to transfer a liability in an orderly transaction between
market patticipants as of the measurement date.

The three levels of the fair value hierarchy ate described as follows:

Level 1 inputs to the valuation methodology ate unadjusted quoted prices for
identical assets ot liabilities in active markets that the Home has the ability to access.

Level 2 inputs to the valuation methodology include: quoted prices for similar assets
ot liabilities in active markets; quoted prices for identical assets ot Kiabilities in
inactive matkets; inputs other than quoted prices that are obsetvable for the asset ot
liability; and, inputs that ate derived ptincipally from or corroborated by obsetvable
market data by cottelation or other means. If the asset ot liability has a specified
(contractual) term, the Level 2 input must be observable for substantially the full
term of the asset or liability.

Level 3 inputs to the valuation methodology are unobsetvable and significant to the
fair value measurement.

Cash and cash equivalents, escrow deposits and replacement reserve

The Home considers all highly liquid investments with original maturities of three -

months ot less to be cash equivalents, excluding money market funds included in the
investment portfolio, which the Home considers to be investments for financial
statement presentation purposes. The Home had cash equivalents of $1,088,000 at
December 31, 2018.

Investments

The Home's investments ate classified as “other than trading secutities” and stated at
fair value. The cost of investments sold is identified using the specific identification
tmethod.

The Home's investments ate ptofessionally managed. Such investments are exposed to
vatious tisks such as intetest rate, market and credit risk. Due to the level of tisk
associated with such investments and the level of uncertainty related to changes in the
value of such investments, it is at least reasonably possible that changes in tisk in the
neat tetm will matetially affect investment balances and the amounts reported in the
consolidated financial statements.

‘The Home evaluates its investments fot other than tempotaty impaitment on an
ongoing basis. A decline in the fair value of an other than trading security below cost
that is deemed to be other than temporaty results in a new cost basis for the secutity
which is equivalent to its fair value. In evaluating other than temporary impaitment, the
Home considets its intent and ability to hold the investment until recovery and other
relevant factots. Duting the year ended December 31, 2018, there wete no such
impaitments.

‘The Home's investments consist of funds designed to provide current income to assist
in the provision of care to the Home's residents and have been designated by the Board
of Ditectors ("Boatd") to function as an endowment. As required by U.S. GAAP, net
assets associated with endowment funds, including funds designated by the Board to
fanction as endowments, ate classified and reported based on the existence ot absence
of donot-imposed testtictions.

Property and equipment (continued

The Home evaluates long-lived assets held and used by the Home for impaitment
whenever events ot changes in citcumstances indicate that the carrying amount of the
asset may not be recovetable. An impaitment loss is recognized if the sum of the
expected undiscounted future cash flows from the use and disposition of the asset is
less than its carrying amount. Generally, the amount of impaitment loss is measuted as
the difference between the cattying amount of the asset and the estimated fair value of
the asset. The Home did not record an impairment loss for the year ended
December 31, 2018.

Debt issuance costs

Debt issuance costs ate being amortized over the term of the telated mottgage note
using the straight-line method, which approximates the effective interest method.
Amottization of debt issuance costs, included with interest expense in the
accompanying consolidated statement of operations, totaled $3,000 for the year ended
December 31, 2018.

The provision for workers’ compensation self-insurance costs includes estimates of the
ultimate costs for both reported claims and claims incurred but not reported.

It is the Home's policy to fund workets' compensation insurance costs acctued.

natural basis in the accompanying consolidated statement of opetations. The
consolidated statement of functional expenses presents the natutal classification detail
of expenses by function. Accordingly, certain costs such as paytoll, payroll taxes and
employee benefits, have been allocated between program, and management and general
based on time and effort.

INVESTMENTS

‘Assets and liabilities are measuted at fait value based on one ot more of the following
valuation techniques:

(a) Market approach: Prices and other relevant information generated by matket
transactions involving identical or comparable assets or liabilities;

(b) Cost approach: Amount that would be requited to teplace the setvice capacity of
an asset (teplacement cost); and

(©) Income approach: Techniques to convert futute amounts to a single present
amount based on market expectations (including present value techniques,
option-pricing and excess earnings models).

COMMITMENT

The Home is a participant in a pooled self-insurance workers! compensation trust (the
"Plan") with six other unrelated not-for-profit nursing and assisted living facilities in the
State of Rhode Island (the "State"). The Plan provides for the payment of premiums
based on the workers' compensation tates established by the State and each facility's
individual expetience. Premiums ate intended to be sufficient to cover all Operating
costs, including claims and tisk management costs, as well as payment for losses.

Each participating facility makes monthly premium payments to the Plan to pay current
costs and losses and to establish a cumulative tesetve for future claims. The tesetve is
held in trust and invested by the Plan's trustee. The Plan maintains excess liability ,
insurance coverage with a commercial insurance cattier to protect the Plan's assets from .
individual losses in excess of $400,000. Each participating facility is jointly and severally:
liable for the claims of the Plan.

North Kingstown, Rhode Island

We have audited the accompanying consolidated financial statements of Scalabrini Villa, Inc. and
Affiliate, which comprise the consolidated balance sheet as of December 31, 2019, and the related

consolidated statements of operations, functional expenses and cash flows for the year then ended, and
the related notes to the consolidated financial statements.

Management's Responsibility for the Financial Statements

Management is responsible for the preparation and fair presentation of these consolidated financial
statements in accordance with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States of America;
this includes the design, implementation, and maintenance of internal control relevant to the

preparation and fair presentation of consolidated financial statements that are free from material
misstatement, whether due to fraud or error.

Auditor's Responsibility

Our responsibility is to express an opinion on these consolidated financial statements based on our
audit. We conducted our audit in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United
States of America. Those standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain reasonable
assurance about whether the consolidated financial statements are free from material misstatement.

An audit involves performing procedures to obtain audit evidence about the amounts and disclosures in
the consolidated financial statements. The procedures selected depend on the auditor's judgment,
including the assessment of the risks of material misstatement of the financial statements, whether due
to fraud or error. In making those tisk assessments, the auditor considers internal control relevant to
the entity's preparation and fair presentation of the financial statements in order to design audit
procedures that are appropriate in the circumstances, but not for the purpose of expressing an opinion
on the effectiveness of the entity's internal control. Accordingly, we express no such opinion. An audit
also includes evaluating the appropriateness of accounting policies used and the reasonableness of

significant accounting estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall presentation of
the financial statements.

We believe that the audit evidence we have obtained is sufficient and appropriate to provide a basis for
our audit opinion.

DESCRIPTION OF ORGANIZATION AND PRINCIPLES OF
CONSOLIDATION

The accompanying consolidated financial statements include the accounts and
transactions of two affiliated entities related through common management: Scalabrini
Villa, Inc. (the "Corporation") and The Pious Society of the Missionaties of St. Charles
Boromeo, Inc. d/b/a Scalabrini Villa (the "Society"). The two entities, which are
referred to herein as the "Home", are governed by the Scalabrini Provincial (the
"Provincial"). The accompanying consolidated financial statements do not include the
accounts and transactions of the Provincial.

The Corporation and the Society are Rhode Island nonstock, nonprofit corporations
organized (sponsored) by The Missionaries of St. Charles. The Society owns a 120-bed
nursing facility located in North Kingstown, Rhode Island. The Corporation operates
the nursing facility, which is leased from the Society.

The Society is regulated by the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development
("HUD") as to operating methods under Section 232 of the National Housing Act of
1959 (the Act"). The Section 232 Housing Program was formed under the Act and
provides HUD-insured mortgage notes to patticipating developments.

All significant intercompany accounts and transactions have been eliminated in the
accompanying consolidated financial statements.

SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES

Recently adopted accounting pronouncements

Revenue recognition

In May 2014, the Financial Accounting Standards Board (“FASB”) issued Accounting
Standards Update (“ASU”) No. 2014-09, Revenue from Contracts with Customers (“Topic
606”), with several, clarifying updates issued subsequently. In conjunction with Topic
606, a new subtopic, Accounting Standards Codification (“ASC”) 340-40, Osher Assets
and Deferred Costs — Contracts with Customers, was also issued. ‘The updated standard
replaces most existing revenue recognition and certain cost guidance under accounting
principles generally accepted in the United States of America (“U.S. GAAP”).
Collectively, we refer to Topic 606 and Subtopic 340-40 as “ASC 606”. ASC 606
amends existing accounting standards for revenue recognition and establishes principles
for recognizing revenue upon the transfer of promised goods or services to customers
based on the expected consideration to be received in exchange for those goods and
setvices. The Home adopted ASC 606 effective January 1, 2019 using the modified
retrospective transition method. Use of the modified retrospective approach means the
Home’s comparative periods prior to initial application are not restated. ‘The Home has
determined that the adjustments using the modified retrospective approach did not have
a material impact on the date of the initial application along with the disclosure of the

effect on prior periods. The Home applied the portfolio approach practical expedient in
implementing ASC 606.

===== PAGE 315 =====
[OCR]
NOTE 2.

SCALABRINI VILLA, INC. AND AFFILIATE
NOTES TO CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS

SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES (CONTINUED)

Recently adopted accounting pronouncements (continued
Financial instraments

In January 2016, the FASB issued ASU No. 2016-01, Financial Instruments-Overall (Subtopic
825-10): Recognition and Measurement of Financial Assets and Financial Liabilities (“ASU 2016-
01”), which changes the classification and measurement of investments in equity
securities and the presentation of certain fair value changes for financial liabilities
measured at fair value. In February 2018, the FASB issued ASU 2018-03, Technical
Corrections and Improvements to Financial Instruments-Overall (Subtopic 825-10): Recognition and
Measurement of Financial Assets and Financial Liabilities ("ASU 2018-03"). In April 2019, the
FASB issued ASU 2019-04, Codification Improvements to Topic 326, Financial Instruments —
Credit Losses, Topic 815, Derivatives and Hedging, and Topic 825, Financial Instruments (“ASU
2019-04”), to clarify certain aspects of 2016-01. The Home adopted the amendments
effective January 1, 2019, using the modified retrospective method. The adoption of
these amendments resulted in the Home including unrealized gains (losses) arising from
the changes in the fair values of equity securities during the period as a component of
investment income, net in the consolidated statement of operations effective January 1,
2019. Previously, such change in unrealized gains (losses) were reported after excess of
revenue over expenses.

Restricted cash

In November 2016, the FASB issued ASU No. 2016-18, Statement of Cash Flows (Topic
230): Restricted Cash (a consensus of the Emenging Issues Task Force) ("ASU 2016-18"), which
required that restricted cash and restricted cash equivalents be included with cash and
cash equivalents when reconciling the beginning-of-period and end-of-period total
amounts shown on the consolidated statement of cash flows. ASU 2016-18 was
effective for annual periods beginning after December 15, 2018. The Home adopted
ASU 2016-18 during the year ended December 31, 2019, and accordingly, the Home
included restricted cash with its cash on the consolidated statement of cash flows.

Use of estimates

The preparation of consolidated financial statements in conformity with U.S. GAAP
requires management to make estimates and assumptions that affect the reported
amounts of assets and liabilities, and disclosure of contingent assets and liabilities at the
date of the financial statements. Estimates also affect the reported amounts of revenue
and expenses during the reporting period. Actual results could differ from those
estimates. The allowance for doubtful accounts is a significant estimate used in the
pteparation of the accompanying consolidated financial statements.

Basis of presentation

The consolidated financial statements of the Home have been prepared in accordance
with U.S. GAAP, which require the Home to report information regarding its financial
position and activities according to the following net asset classifications:

===== PAGE 316 =====

NOTE 2.

SCALABRINI VILLA, INC. AND AFFILIATE

SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES (CONTINUED)
Basis of presentation (continued)

Net Assets Without Donor Restrictions - Net assets available for use in genetal operations
and not subject to donor (or certain grantor) restrictions.

Net Assets With Donor Restrictions - Net assets subject to donor-imposed restrictions.
Some donor-imposed restrictions are temporary in nature, such as those that will be met
by the passage of time or other events specified by the donor. Other donor-imposed
restrictions are perpetual in nature, where the donor stipulates that resources be
maintained in perpetuity. Gifts of long-lived assets and gifts of cash restricted for the
acquisition of long-lived assets are recognized as revenue when the assets are placed in

setvice. Net assets with donor restrictions at December 31, 2019 are restricted for the
memorial garden.

The Home teports contributions restricted by donors as increases in net assets without
donor restrictions if the restrictions expire (that is, when a stipulated time restriction
ends of purpose restriction is accomplished) in the reporting period in which the
revenue is recognized. When a restriction expires, net assets with donor restrictions are
reclassified to net assets without donor restrictions and reported in the consolidated
statement of operations as net assets released from testrictions.

Revenue, gains and other support are reported as increases in net assets without donor
restrictions unless use of the related assets is limited by donor-imposed restrictions or
by law. Expenses are reported as decreases in net assets without donor restrictions.
Gains and losses on investments or other assets or liabilities are reported as increases or

decreases in net assets without donor restrictions unless their use is restricted by explicit
donor stipulation or by law.

Excess of revenue over Expenses

The consolidated statement of operations includes excess of revenue over expenses.
Changes in net assets without donor restrictions which are excluded from excess of

revenue over expenses, consistent with industry practice, include the change in net
unrealized gains on debt securities.

Contributions

Contribution revenue is recorded as received or unconditionally promised. If a
contribution is made in assets other than cash, the amount of the contribution is
measured at the fair value of the asset at the date the contribution or unconditional
promise to give is made by the donor.

Contributed services

Contributed services received by the Home are not recognized as support since such

services either do not requite specialized skills or would not typically be purchased if not
provided by donation.

===== PAGE 317 =====
[OCR]
NOTE 2.

SCALABRINI VILLA, INC. AND AFFILIATE
NOTES TO CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
DECEMBER 31, 2019

SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES (CONTINUED)

atient service revenue

Revenue recognition

The Home adopted ASC Topic 606, Revenue from Contracts with Customers (“Topic 606”)
on January 1, 2019. Revenue is measured based on consideration specified in a contract
with a customer, and excludes any amounts collected on behalf of third parties. The
Home recognizes revenue when it satisfies a performance obligation by transferring
conttol over a product or service to a customer. The majority of the Home’s revenue
originates from contracts with customers to provide various healthcare services. The
majority of the Home’s services represent a bundle of services that are not capable of
being distinct and as such, are treated as a single performance obligation that is satisfied
over time as the services are rendered. The Home’s standard payment terms are within

30 days from billing.

The Home determines the transaction price based on contractually agreed upon rates,
adjusted for estimates of variable consideration, such as implicit price concessions.
Variable consideration also exists as the Home’s third-party revenue is paid principally
at prospectively determined rates. Retroactive adjustments are considered in the
recognition of revenue on an estimated basis in the period the related services ate
rendered such that net patient service revenues are only recorded to the extent that it is
probable that a significant reversal in the amount of cumulative revenue recognized will
not occur in the future. Any differences between the estimated amounts accrued, and
interim and final settlements are adjusted in future periods as adjustments become
known or as years are no longer subject to such audits, reviews and investigations.

Disaggregation of revenues

The Home operates as a skilled nursing facility and provides services to patients, who
have third-party financial coverage (principally Rhode Island Medicaid and Medicare), as
well as to other patients who are responsible for their own financial obligations.
Therefore, the Home’s viability is dependent on a number of factors such as licensed
bed capacity, occupancy rates, mix of patients by payor and case type, the rates of
reimbursement, changes to Medicaid and Medicare programs, and the Home’s ability to
collect on its contracts. All of the Home’s net patient service revenue for financial
reporting purposes are recorded over time as the services are rendered.

Net patient service revenue from third-party payors totaled $ 7,750,000 for the year
ended December 31, 2019. Net patient service revenue from private payors totaled
$ 2,328,000 for the year ended December 31, 2019.

Significant risks and uncertainties

Laws and regulations governing Medicare, Medicaid and other governmental
reimbursement programs are extremely complex and subject to interpretation. As a
result, there is at least a reasonable possibility that recorded estimates could change by a
material amount in the near term, It is management’s opinion that the Home is in
compliance with applicable laws and regulations; however, the possibility of government
review and interpretation, as well as significant regulatory action; failure to comply with
such laws and regulations can result in fines, penalties and exclusion from Medicare and
Medicaid programs.

NOTE 2.

SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES (CONTINUED)

Accounts receivable

Patient accounts receivable are stated at the amount the Home expects to collect.

Under Rhode Island General Law Title 40, Chapter 8, Section 6.1(b)(2), Human Services,
Medical Assistance, Provider Care During Pendency of Application, Uncompensated Care While
Determination is Overdue, upon the Home’s request, the State of Rhode Island (the
“State”) is required to make payments to the Home for charges related to a patient that
the Home has determined should be eligible for Medicaid and for whom a completed
application (as defined) for Medicaid benefits filed with the State has been pending for
90 days or more (“advance payments”); such advance payments may not be recovered,
offset or recouped by the State upon a subsequent determination by the State that the
patient is not eligible for Medicaid. Consequently, advance payments received by the
Home prior to the date the State has made a determination related to the patient’s
eligibility for Medicaid are offset against the patient services receivable in the
accompanying consolidated balance sheet. Advance payments received by the Home
related to Medicaid claims subsequently approved and paid by the State are being
recouped on a monthly basis, readjusted quarterly, through June 2021 and,
consequently, ate reported within current and long-term liabilities, third-party advances
in the accompanying consolidated balance sheet, totaling $472,000. At December 31,

2019, advance payments offset against Medicaid receivables totaled $182,000. During
2019, the Home repaid the State $676,000.

The Home provides for probable uncollectible amounts through a charge to provision
for losses on accounts receivable and a credit to the allowance for doubtful accounts
based on its assessment of the current status of individual accounts. Balances that are
still outstanding after the Home has used reasonable collection efforts are written off
through a charge to the allowance for doubtful accounts and a credit to receivables.

The Home does not obtain collateral for amounts due. At December 31, 2019,

approximately 58% of the Home’s net accounts receivable is due from Rhode Island
Medicaid.

At December 31, 2019 and 2018, net receivables was $ 486,000 and $ 656,000 ,
respectively,

Fair value measurements

FASB ASC 820, Fair Value Measurement, establishes a framework for measuring fair
value. That framework provides a fair value hierarchy that prioritizes the inputs to
valuation techniques used to measure fair value. The hierarchy gives the highest priority
to unadjusted quoted prices in active markets for identical assets or liabilities (Level 1)
and the lowest priority to unobservable inputs (Level 3). Categorization within the
valuation hieratchy is based upon the lowest level of input that is significant to the fair
value measurement. Valuation techniques used need to maximize the use of observable
inputs and minimize the use of unobservable inputs. Under the standard, fair value is
defined as the exit price, or the amount that would be received to sell an asset or paid to

transfer a liability in an orderly transaction between market participants as of the
measurement date.

11

===== PAGE 319 =====
[OCR]
NOTE 2.

SCALABRINI VILLA, INC. AND AFFILIATE
NOTES TO CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
DECEMBER 31, 2019

SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES (CONTINUED)
Fair value measurements (continued)

The thtee levels of the fair value hierarchy are described as follows:

Level 1 inputs to the valuation methodology are unadjusted quoted prices for
identical assets or liabilities in active markets that the Home has the ability to access.

Level 2 inputs to the valuation methodology include: quoted prices for similar assets
or liabilities in active markets; quoted prices for identical assets or liabilities in
inactive markets; inputs other than quoted prices that are observable for the asset or
liability; and, inputs that are derived principally from or corroborated by observable
market data by correlation or other means. If the asset or liability has a specified
(contractual) term, the Level 2 input must be observable for substantially the full
term of the asset or liability.

Level 3 inputs to the valuation methodology are unobservable and significant to the
fair value measurement.

Cash and cash equivalents, escrow deposits and replacement reserve

The Home considers all highly liquid investments with original maturities of three
months or less to be cash equivalents, excluding money market funds included in the
investment portfolio, which the Home considers to be investments for financial
statement presentation purposes. The Home had cash equivalents of $439,000 at
December 31, 2019.

The Home maintains its cash and cash equivalents in accounts with two financial
institutions whose deposits are insured by the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation
("FDIC"). At December 31, 2019, the Home had $353,000 on deposit in excess of
amounts insured by the FDIC.

The mortgagee holds the mortgage escrow deposits (noninterest-bearing) and
replacement reserve (interest-bearing) in accounts insured by the FDIC at two financial
institutions. At December 31, 2019, these deposits exceeded the insured limits by
$316,000.

Investments

The Home's investments are stated at fair value. The cost of investments sold is
identified using the specific identification method. Included in investment income, net
on the consolidated statement of operations is $648,000 related to the change in net
unrealized gains from equity securities that are held at December 31, 2019.

The Home's investments are professionally managed. Such investments are exposed to
various risks such as interest rate, market and credit risk. Due to the level of risk
associated with such investments and the level of uncertainty related to changes in the
value of such investments, it is at least reasonably possible that changes in risk in the
near term will materially affect investment balances and the amounts reported in the
consolidated financial statements.

SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES (CONTINUED)
Investments (continued)

The Home evaluates its debt securities for other than temporary impairment on an
ongoing basis. A decline in the fair value of a debt security below cost that is deemed to
be other than temporary results in a new cost basis for the security which is equivalent
to its fair value. In evaluating other than temporary impairment, the Home considets its
intent and ability to hold the investment until recovery and other relevant factors.
During the year ended December 31, 2019, there were no such impairments.

The Home's investments consist of funds designed to provide current income to assist
in the provision of care to the Home's residents and have been designated by the Board
of Directors ("Board") to function as an endowment. As required by U.S. GAAP, net
assets associated with endowment funds, including funds designated by the Board to

function as endowments, are classified and reported based on the existence or absence
of donor-imposed restrictions.

Property and equipment

Property and equipment ate stated at cost, less accumulated depreciation. Depreciation
expense totaled $353,000 for the year ended December 31, 2019.

Depreciation is provided using the straight-line method over the estimated useful lives
of the assets, which are as follows:

Equipment 3-25 years
Buildings and improvements 5-40 years

The Home evaluates long-lived assets held and used by the Home for impairment
whenever events or changes in circumstances indicate that the carrying amount of the
asset may not be recoverable. An impairment loss is recognized if the sum of the
expected undiscounted future cash flows from the use and disposition of the asset is
less than its catrying amount. Generally, the amount of impairment loss is measured as
the difference between the carrying amount of the asset and the estimated fair value of

the asset. The Home did not record an impairment loss for the year ended
December 31, 2019.

Debt issuance costs

Debt issuance costs are being amortized over the term of the related mortgage note
using the straight-line method, which approximates the effective interest method.
Amortization of debt issuance costs, included with interest expense in the

accompanying consolidated statement of operations, totaled $3,000 for the year ended
December 31, 2019.

Workers' compensation self-insurance costs

The provision for workers' compensation self-insurance costs includes estimates of the
ultimate costs for both reported claims and claims incurred but not reported.

It is the Home's policy to fund workers' compensation insurance costs accrued.

NOTE 4. INVESTMENTS AND SUBSEQUENT EVENT

Assets and liabilities are measured at fair value based on one or mote of the following
valuation techniques:

(a) Market approach: Prices and other relevant information generated by market
transactions involving identical or comparable assets or liabilities;

(b) Cost approach: Amount that would be required to replace the service capacity of
an asset (replacement cost); and

(c) Income approach: Techniques to convert future amounts to a single present
amount based on market expectations (including present value techniques,
option-pricing and excess earnings models).

Following is a description of the valuation methodologies used for assets at fair value.
Cash and money matket funds

Cash and money market funds are valued at the deposit amount plus accrued interest,
which approximates fair value and are classified as Level 1.

U.S. Government agency obligations

US. Government agency obligations are valued using pricing models, such as matrix
pricing with inputs that are observable in the market or can be derived principally from
ot corroborated by observable market data and are classified as Level 1.

Corporate and foreign bonds

Corporate and foreign bonds are valued using pricing models, such as matrix pricing
with inputs that are observable in the market or can be derived principally from or
cotroborated by observable market data and ate classified as Level 2.

Equities

Equities are valued at the closing price from the relevant stock exchange and are
classified as Level 1.

Mutual funds

Mutual funds are valued at quoted market prices in active markets, which represent the
net asset value of the funds and are classified as Level 1.

COMMITMENT

The Home is a participant in a pooled self-insurance workers' compensation trust (the
"Plan") with six other unrelated not-for-profit nursing and assisted living facilities in the
State of Rhode Island (the "State"). The Plan provides for the payment of premiums
based on the workers' compensation rates established by the State and each facility's
individual experience. Premiums are intended to be sufficient to cover all operating
costs, including claims and risk management costs, as well as payment for losses.

Each participating facility makes monthly premium payments to the Plan to pay current
costs and losses and to establish a cumulative reserve for future claims. ‘The reserve is
held in trust and invested by the Plan's trustee. The Plan maintains excess liability
insurance coverage with a commercial insurance cartier to protect the Plan's assets from
individual losses in excess of $400,000. Each participating facility is jointly and severally
liable for the claims of the Plan.

18

===== PAGE 326 =====

NOTE 9.

SCALABRINI VILLA, INC. AND AFFILIATE

DECEMBER 31, 2019

COMMITMENT (CONTINUED)

The Plan trustee maintains separate accountability for each participating facility's
portion of contributions, costs, losses and cumulative reserve for future claims. Should
any facility's premiums be inadequate for its share of claims, costs and losses, the
remaining facilities’ portion of the Plan assets can be used to satisfy the payments
allocated to that facility. In the event of Plan termination, any assets remaining in the
Plan in excess of amounts needed to pay claims, costs and losses will be ultimately
returned to the participating facilities based on their pro rata shares.

The Plan's premiums totaled approximately $820,000 (unaudited) for the year ended
December 31, 2019, of which the Home's estimated portion totaled $110,000. The
Plan's net assets, as provided by the Plan trustee, amounted to approximately $2,806,000
(unaudited) at December 31, 2019. The Plan trustee has not provided financial

information regarding claims incurred but not reported and claims reported but not
settled as of December 31, 2019.

RELATED-PARTY TRANSACTIONS

The Home has a supervisory agreement with Scalabrini Provincial in which the
Provincial Superior will provide supervisory services and oversight of the Home for
$5,000 per quarter. $5,000 is included in accounts payable in the accompanying
consolidated balance sheet. Included in contracted services in the accompanying
consolidated statement of operations is $20,000 for these services.

MEDICAID ELIGIBILITY DETERMINATION DELAYS

Since early 2016, the State has been significantly delayed in rendering Medicaid eligibility
determinations with respect to patients that the Home has determined should be eligible
for Medicaid and for whom a completed application for Medicaid benefits has been
filed with the State. The Home provides services to these patients and recognizes
related revenue during the period in which the State has not made a determination
related to a completed application filed with the State ("Medicaid Pending Application").
Amounts reported as patient services receivables related to Medicaid Pending
Applications that have been pending for less than 90 days and those that have been
pending for 90 days or more for which the Home has not requested an advance
payment (see Note 2) may be subject to subsequent denial by the State at some future
date. Management believes that the State will ultimately determine that patients for
whom a Medicaid Pending Application exists are eligible for Medicaid and that any
potential loss related to Medicaid Pending Applications subsequently denied by the State
will not have a material adverse effect on the Home’s financial position.

CONTINGENCY

The Corporation is involved in a number of malpractice claims arising out of the
conduct of its business, the outcomes of which are uncertain. These claims have been
referred to the Corporation's malpractice insurance carrier. No amounts have been
recorded in the accompanying consolidated financial statements relating to these
uncertainties. It is reasonably possible that the outcomes of these matters could be
determined in the near term; however, management does not believe these matters will
ultimately have a material adverse effect on the Corporation.

UNCERTAINTY

In 2020, the World Health Organization has declared COVID-19 to constitute a “Public
Health Emergency of International Concern.” Disruptions to the Home’s business
operations could occur as a result from the supply and quarantine of employees,
residents, and vendors. The Home has experienced a decline in admissions and census
and additional costs associated with staffing and protective medical supplies due to the
COVID-19 pandemic. As a response, the Home has tequested funds from its
investment portfolio (see Note 4), requested a 3-month advance from Medicare on
futute billings and submitted a loan application under the Paycheck Protection Program
with its bank as part of the Small Business Act. Given the uncertainty of this situation,
the financial impact to the Home, if any, cannot be reasonably estimated at this time.
